# Technical Report: A Formal Semantics for Java Symbolic Evaluation using Large-Block Encoding 

## Soha Hussein 1* , Stephen McCamant 2, Kelton OBrien 3,Kuen-Bang Hou (Favonia) 2, Michael Whalen 4, Vaibhav Sharma 4

> 1*

Faculty of Computer and Information Sciences, Ain Shams University, Egypt. 

> 2

Faculty of Computer Science and Engineering, University of Minnesota, USA. 

> 3

Luddy School of Informatics, Computing, and Engineering, Indiana University, USA. 

> 4

Amazon Web Services, USA. *Corresponding author(s). E-mail(s): soha.hussein@cis.asu.edu.eg; Contributing authors: mccamant@cs.umn.edu; kelpobri@iu.edu; kbh@umn.edu; mww@amazon.com; svaib@amazon.com; 

Abstract 

Symbolic execution plays a critical role in software reliability, as they are used to find bugs, generate test cases, and provide correctness guarantees, particularly for safety-critical systems. Yet their own correctness is rarely subject to formal scrutiny, as it is typically established empirically by evaluating tool behavior across many programs. This leaves open the possibility that the tools them-selves introduce unsoundness, potentially invalidating the verification results they produce and undermining the very guarantees they are meant to provide. In this paper, we address this gap by providing the formal treatment of sym-bolic execution with path-merging, an optimization that improves path explosion by summarizing branching code regions into disjunctive constraints rather than exploring each path independently. Specifically, we target Java Ranger, a path-merging tool for Java programs that progressively transforms imperative Java code toward the language of formal logic through a series of code transforma-tions. We formalize each of these transformations and prove their soundness with respect to a simplified version of the Java concrete semantics, establishing that Java Ranger’s path-merging process preserves program semantics. 

1arXiv:2608.04513v1 [cs.SC] 5 Aug 2026 Keywords: Symbolic Execution, Path-Merging, Program Analysis, Compilers, Program Proofs: Technical Report 

# 1 Introduction 

Symbolic execution (SE) [1] is a formal testing and verification technique that ensures reliability and safety of programs, particularly for critical software where correctness guarantees are essential. Rather than executing programs on concrete inputs, SE treats inputs as symbolic values and explores program paths by collecting path conditions; i.e., logical constraints that characterize the inputs that reach each path. This general-ity makes SE applicable to a wide range of tasks including test input generation [2, 3], program and specification repair [4, 5], equivalence checking [6, 7], vulnerability finding [8, 9], invariant discovery [10], and protocol correctness checking [11]. Despite the widespread use of symbolic execution for assuring critical software, surprisingly little attention has been paid to formally establishing the correctness of the tools themselves. This creates a peculiar situation: we rely on symbolic execution to verify programs, yet the verifiers remain unverified. In practice, confidence in these tools is built empirically by running them on many programs and observing their behavior over time. While this approach can surface errors that manifest as visible failures (crashes or obviously incorrect results), it provides no formal guarantees about the tool’s correctness. The benchmarking practices in the field reflect this empirical approach. The Soft-ware Verification Competition (SV-COMP) [12] provides a large suite of programs with known verdicts against which tools are tested. Notably, the competition’s scoring scheme explicitly anticipates unsound behavior: a tool is penalized 16 points for incor-rectly reporting a safe program as unsafe (false positive), and 32 points for incorrectly reporting an unsafe program as safe (false negative) [13]. The asymmetric penalties acknowledge that claiming safety incorrectly is more dangerous than spurious warn-ings, but the very existence of these penalties assumes that tools will produce wrong answers. This assumption is borne out in practice. In SV-COMP 2026, among 7 par-ticipating tools for Java verification, 4 tools produced a combined total of 21 incorrect verdicts: GDart (2), JBMC (14), jLiSA (1), and MLB (4). It is crucial to distinguish between two fundamentally different sources of incor-rect verdicts. First, bounded search (depth limits, timeouts) may prevent complete exploration. A ”safe” verdict could be sound for all explored paths but incomplete for the full program. Second, tools may contain flaws in their core reasoning: bugs in modeling language semantics, program translation, or constraint solving. Here, even explored paths are analyzed incorrectly. Without formal semantics, these failure modes are indistinguishable, if at all observable. Users cannot tell whether ”safe” means the bug was unreached or incorrectly analyzed. Formal semantics and soundness proofs address the second problem (the focus of our paper): ensuring that whatever a tool analyzes, it analyzes correctly. Ironically, even establishing ground truth for verification benchmarks is challenging due to a circular dependency: benchmarks rely on tools to reveal bugs, yet we need 2correct verdicts to evaluate whether tools work correctly. This is reflected in practice: verification tasks are often added to SV-COMP with provisional verdicts, which are later revised when tools discover previously undetected bugs. Even tools with strong empirical track records can harbor subtle soundness bugs that remain undetected for years. Java-Ranger won Gold Medals at SV-COMP in 2020, 2021, and 2025, yet during this work, we discovered a bug that had existed since its inception in 2020. The bug resided in field transformation: when a field operation followed a method invocation, the SSA construction failed to account for possible side effects on the field value, producing incorrect summaries. This bug was never caught by test suites or competition evaluations. It only surfaced when we attempted to prove soundness of the transformation and the proof failed. This exemplifies what formal verification of tools provides: bugs that are invisible to empirical testing become provably impossible when the tool’s correctness is established formally. In this paper, we address this gap by targeting the most semantically complex com-ponent of symbolic execution tools, where the risk of unsoundness is highest. Formally verifying a complete symbolic execution engine end-to-end is an ambitious undertaking that remains an open challenge, but a natural first step is to focus on the component whose incorrectness would have the most severe consequences. We therefore target Java Ranger (JR), a symbolic execution tool with path-merging for Java programs. JR operates by identifying regions of code where branching is about to occur dur-ing dynamic exploration, and attempts to collapse each such region into a disjunctive predicate that collectively describes the behavior of all paths through it. This summa-rization is achieved through a sequence of systematic transformations , each of which abstracts away one language feature, progressively moving the representation of the region from imperative Java toward the language of formal logic. A bug in any one of these transformations could silently corrupt the summary, causing JR to report verdicts that do not reflect the behavior of the original program. We therefore formal-ize each of JR’s transformations as operating on an intermediate representation (IR) and prove their soundness with respect to the concrete semantics of the IR. Through the proof, we establish that the produced summaries faithfully preserve program se-mantics. Although the formalization we provide in this paper is not machine-checked, we view this as a foundational step toward a broader formal treatment of symbolic execution tools. The remainder of the paper is organized as follows. Section 2 presents an overview of Java Ranger and its transformations. Section 3 presents related work and Section 4 presents the concrete semantics of the bytecode-like language that we use for prov-ing soundness of JR transformations. Section 5 presents the underlying assumptions and structure of traditional symbolic execution and Section 6 presents the initial state of Java Ranger and its environment. The subsequent sections (Section 6.2-Section 14) formalize and prove the soundness of each transformation in turn: 

γ-creation (Section 6.2), which identifies the region of interest for summarization; early-return elimination (Section 8 and Section 9), which normalizes control flow by removing intermediate returns; variable renaming (Section 10), which ensures variable names are unique across paths; variable substitution (Section 11), which propagates variable definitions forward through the region; method inlining (Section 12), which 3eliminates method invocations by substituting their definitions; and field SSA creation (Section 13), which converts field operations into SSA form while accounting for pos-sible side effects of method invocations. Finally, Section 15 brings the transformations together, presents the Java Ranger fixpoint algorithm, and proves the soundness of the overall path-merging process. 

# 2 Java Ranger Overview 

Java Ranger is a path-merging symbolic execution for Java programs that operates by identifying regions of code where branching is about to occur during dynamic exploration, and attempts to collapse each such region into a disjunctive predicate that collectively describes the behavior of all paths through it. This summarization is achieved through a sequence of systematic transformations , each of which abstracts away one language feature, progressively moving the representation of the region from imperative Java toward the language of formal logic. The transformation pipeline proceeds as follows. The higher-order transformation eliminates method invocations by inlining their definitions into the region. The field 

transformation eliminates heap references by introducing Static Single Assignment (SSA) local variables that reflect the value or computation associated with each ref-erence. Finally, single-path-cases identifies constructs that resist summarization, such as heap allocations and exceptions, and marks them as exit points to be explored in-dividually. Together, these transformations produce a disjunctive constraint that Java Ranger submits to the underlying constraint solver in place of enumerating each path individually. At any point during the summarization, if any of the transformations fails, then JR reverts to using traditional SE exploring each path separately. For example, consider the code in Listing 1. The code takes in an integer and it separates the number of set bits from the remaining bits. For example, for i = 20 the output is 11000000, assuming that i is an 8-bit integer. To do that, the program runs in a loop while i is not equal zero. In each iteration, it checks if the rightmost bit is one 

line 4 . If yes, then the program performs an unsigned right shift, sets the leftmost bit on (line 10) , and removes the rightmost bit in i (line 11) . Otherwise, the program tries to find the number of rightmost trailing zeros, and then shifts i by that number 

(lines 7, 9) .                                                        

> 1public int separateBits ( int i) {
> 2int j=9;
> 3while (i != 0) {
> 4int hasZeroTrail =(i &1);
> 5if ( hasZeroTrail == 0) {
> 6int numOfZeros =Integer . numTrailZero (i );
> 7i=i>> numOfZeros ;
> 8}else {
> 9j=j>>> 1;
> 10 j=j*Integer . reverse (1);
> 11 i=i>> 1;
> 12 }}
> 13 return j; }
> Listing 1: Example: separates the number of set bits from the remaining bits

4To analyze the program with symbolic execution, at each iteration of the while loop operating over a single bit, there are two possibilities that the program needs to analyze. This results in a number of paths that are exponential in the number of bits in i, i.e., 2 32 paths for an unrestricted 32-bit i. Using JR one can explore the same program by collapsing branches inside the while loop (lines 5-12) , as well as those that exist in numTrailZero , resulting in exploring a maximum of 16 paths for the same input. JR operates in two phases, a static phase, and a dynamic phase. The static phase analyzes the code to be executed and identifies potential regions of code that could be merged. The dynamic phase attempts to summarize a specific code region into a disjunctive constraint. It does so by translating the Java features through a sequence of transformations. We now talk in detail about these two phases. 

## 2.1 Static Phase 

During the static phase, the region’s to-be-merged code is first recovered into a state-ment in JR’s intermediate representation (IR), and processed through a series of static transformations, i.e., transformations that are not dependent on the dynamic val-ues/objects. In general, Java Ranger starts by creating the Control Flow Graph (CFG) of the program’s bytecode. It then scans the graph to find basic blocks with condi-tional instructions, i.e., a basic block with two successors. If one is found, it translates the basic blocks between the conditional instruction and its immediate post dominator as an if-statement in JR’s IR. JR avoids recovering statements with loops. This is done by disregarding a sub-graph whose post-dominator that is also a predecessor of a node. While JR will disregard recovering this subgraph, it will attempt to recover other subgraphs within the identified loop. In other words, JR does not attempt to summarize loops but can summarize statements within a loop if no inner loop exists. For example, the recovered JR IR-statement for the example in Listing 1 is:                                          

> 1if ( w7 != 0) {
> 2w11 := w19 >>> 1;
> 3w13 := invoke ( w13 , Integer , reverse (I)I ,1)
> 4w14 := w11 ˆw13 ;
> 5w15 := w18 >> 1;
> 6}else {
> 7invoke (w9 , Integer , numTrailZero (I)I , w18 )
> 8w10 := w18 >> 1; };
> 9ϕ( w16 , w15 , w10 );
> 10 ϕ( w17 , w14 , w19 );
> Listing 2: Initial IR statement for seperateBits

Where w7 refers to hasZeroT rail variable, the first definition of i is w18, and finally the first definition of j is w19. For example, the if-statement in Listing 2 at line 1 has two trailing ϕ-statements (ϕ(w16 , w15 , w10) and ϕ(w17 , w14 , w19)) capturing the changes to the i variable in Listing 1 in lines 7,11 , and the changes to the j variable also in the same figure at 9, 10 , respectively. In the γ-creation transformation, these ϕ-statements are replaced with assignment statements with γ-expressions. For example, lines 10,11 

in Listing 3 shows the corresponding assignment statements created for these ϕ-statements.

On the other hand, the return path constraint describes all relative conditions that can exist within a region. the a var. ## 2.2 Dynamic Phase 

In this phase, JR uses the state of the dynamic environment in an attempt to elim-inate additional Java language features, such as method invocation, field operations and exceptions. The idea here is that using the dynamic state JR can reduce the pos-sibilities of dispatched methods, fields and specific exceptions. This phase consists of four main transformations 1. Local Variable Substitution : The substitution transformation substitutes values of the region’s inputs that are defined in I. In this example, I is defined to contain 

w7, w18 and w19 with instantiation-time values being the symbolic expressions ia & 1, 

ia, and 0 respectively.                                                        

> 1if ( i_a &1!= 0){
> 2( w11 ,1) := 0>>> 1;
> 3invoke (( w13 ,1) , Integer , reverse (I)I ,1)
> 4( w14 ,1) := ( w11 ,1) ˆ( w13 ,1);
> 5( w15 ,1) := i_a >> 1;
> 6}else {
> 7invoke (( w9 ,1) , Integer , numTrailZero (I)I , i_a )
> 8( w10 ,1) := i_a >> 1; }
> 9( w16 ,1) := γ(( i_a &1) !=0 , ( w15 ,1) , ( w10 ,1));
> 10 ( w17 ,1) := γ(( i_a &1) !=0 , ( w14 ,1) , 0);
> Listing 7: IR for seperateBits after substitution

2. Method Inlining : To enable method inlining two main steps need to occur. The first binds the passed parameters to the invoked method arguments, the second captures the return value if one exists as an assignment in the caller. This trans-formation also inlines recursive functions up to a preconfigured depth. For example 7listing 8 shows inlining, where ( w9, 1) holds the return expression from invoking numTrailZero.                                                          

> 1if (( i_a &1) != 0){
> 2( w11 ,1) := 0>>> 1;
> 3...
> 4}else {
> 5if (!( i_a != 0)) {
> 6skip ;
> 7}else {
> 8skip ; }
> 9...
> 10 (w9 ,1) := γ(!( i_a !=0) , 32 , else ( w29 ,3));
> 11 ( w10 ,1) := i_a >> 1; }
> 12 ( w16 ,1) := γ( i_a &1!=0 , ( w15 ,1) , ( w10 ,1));
> 13 ( w17 ,1) := γ( i_a &1!=0 , ( w14 ,1) , 0);
> Listing 8: IR for numT railZero after method-inlining

3. Field and Arrays GSA Creation : The intuition behind this transformation is that, all fields updates can replaced with some fresh ssa local variable that are uniquely indexed. Likewise all fields look-ups are replaced with the corresponding ssa variable. Creating these local variables and using them sensibly to reflect field updates and lookups, is the purpose of this transformation. Therefore the output of this transformation is a bunch of local variable assignments, with no field lookup or update statements. 4. Single Path Cases : Java Ranger avoids summarizing object creation and excep-tions due to some architectural limitations. However, giving up on path-merging of all code regions that are enclosing object creations or exceptions, would mean that Java Ranger will give up on potentially many useful merges. Thus, we have intro-duced the Single Path Cases transformation. In this transformation, Java Ranger identifies two constraints, the first capturing the relative conditions of statements it can summarize, while the second capturing the relative conditions of state-ments that it cannot summarize, and where traditional symbolic execution needs to dynamically execute. In our motivational example, JR was able to summarize all the code region. However introducing a print statements on the else side would result in two pred-icates, one that describe the summary of the code region without the branch that contains the print statement, and another that is used to target the execution of SSE to it. 5. Linearization : In this transformation, if-statements are removed, and state-ments on the then and the else side are composed in a sequential composition. This is possible now at this transformation because all local variable assignments are guarded, i.e., are captured by the condition in γ-statements. 81 ( w15 ,1) := i_a >> 1 ;                                                          

> 2(w7 ,3) := i_a << 16 ;
> 3(w9 ,3) := γ(( w7 ,3)!=0 , (w7 ,3) , i_a );
> 4( w10 ,3) := γ(( w7 ,3)!=0 , 15 , 31);
> 5( w12 ,3) := (w9 ,3) << 8;
> 6( w13 ,3) := ( w10 ,3) -8;
> 7...
> 8(w9 ,1) := γ(( i_a ==0) ,32 , ( w29 ,3));
> 9( w10 ,1) := i_a >> 1;
> 10 ( w16 ,1) := γ( i_a &1!=0 , ( w15 ,1) , ( w10 ,1));
> 11 ( w17 ,1) := γ( i_a &1!=0 , -2147483648 , 0);
> Listing 9: IR’s snippet for numT railZero after linearization

After all the transformations are executed successfully, Java Ranger proceeds as follows. First, JR will create a conjunctive constraint of all the expressions that resulted from the linearization transformation. Then, it will create an execution path to explore the unsummarized behavior from the single path cases, as well as, another one to explore returns from the early return transformation. Finally, Java Ranger will set up the state of variables in each case, and will allow SE to resume execution by exploring these paths. Next, we will show the formal semantics of JR’s transformations, and the soundness proofs of the main transformations. The general form of the proof of the soundness of JR is to establish correspondence between any of JR transformations and the execution of the concrete semantics. For if JR is sound with respect to concrete semantics, then using JR in a symbolic execution would be sound as long as the symbolic execution is sound and correct. Thus in the rest of the paper is organized as follows: first we present the concrete semantics which we will use to show the soundness of JR’s transformations, next, we will present the semantics of main JR’s transformations along with their soundeness proofs, and finally, present the overall algorithm of JR. 

# 3 Related Work 

Path explosion is one of the most commonly observed scalability challenges of symbolic execution, and works prior to Java Ranger have explored a variety of path merging approaches. In symbolic execution tools that represent multiple complete symbolic execution states, one natural approach is to implement a merging operation on these state representations. Hansen et al. [14] and Kuznetsov et al. [15] explored such state-merging techniques, and MultiSE [16] achieves a similar effect within a somewhat different tool architecture. By contrast, Java Ranger takes a different approach of statically summarizing code regions with branching control-flow, more closely related to the Veritesting approach of Avgerinos et al. [17] in their system MergePoint for binary executables. The static summarization approach is easier to adopt in a symbolic execution tool that is designed to have only on execution state at once, like Symbolic PathFinder (SPF) [18] that Java Ranger builds upon. Java Ranger is significantly different from MergePoint because of the differences between binary code and Java bytecode: for instance Java Ranger 9can take advantage of structured control-flow, but needs to perform its own method inlining because of the prevalence of small methods in Java. Many of the invariants (such as SSA form) and transformations (such as method inlining and constant propagation) used by Java Ranger are similar to techniques used in compilers, and compilers are the language transformation tools for which correctness proof techniques are most developed. The two main classes of verification tools for compilers are translation validators [19–21], which check the correctness of a particular compilation run, and certified compilers, such a CompCert [22] for C and CakeML [23] for Standard ML, which are proven once-and-for-all to compile any input program correctly. Our proofs about Java Ranger are once-and-for-all, but in being transformations within an intermediate representation, they are similar to correctness verification of individual compiler passes [24–27]. A number of previous formal descriptions of symbolic execution have been language-agnostic or used simple languages for clarity. Schwartz et al. [28] use a sim-ple formal language to explain taint analysis and symbolic execution, though they also mention a number of practical implementation concerns. De Boer and Bonsangue [29] use a transition system model to formalize symbolic execution for a sequence of languages of increasing complexity. In a recent work, Voogd et al. [30] present a deno-tational semantics for symbolic execution along with a machine-checked formalization. The formalization of Porncharoenwase et al. [31] has the closest purpose to ours in introducing an abstract form of state merging, which generalizes path merging and can be applied to code with loops and recursion. They described a machine-checked implementation for a core subset of Scheme and more realistic optimized tool that is tested against it. 

# 4 Semantics of Concrete Execution 

In this section, we define the concrete execution semantics that will be used to establish the soundness of JR’s transformations. The intermediate representation employed by JR is based on a variation of the Java bytecode without the operand stack, expressed in single static assignment (SSA) form [32], where each variable is assigned exactly once. 

## 4.1 Concrete Grammar 

Figure 1 presents the grammar of JR’s IR. The metavariables used throughout the semantics range over the following domains. Integers n ∈ Z and positive subscripts 

j ∈ Z+ ⊎{⊥} are used to index and distinguish program variables, where ⊥ denotes the absence of a subscript. Variable identifiers a ∈ Id name program variables, field names 

f ∈ F denote object fields, class names c ∈ C range over the set of declared classes, method signatures g ∈ G identify methods, and references r ∈ R denote concrete heap addresses. Note that references are always concrete and are never permitted to take symbolic values. The expression language of the grammar includes standard literals, variables, and both unary and binary expressions. In addition, it features the γ-expression, which 10 integer n ∈ Z subscript j ∈ Z+ ⊎ {⊥} identif ier a ∈ Id f ield f ∈ Fclass c ∈ C signature g ∈ G ref erence r ∈ Rliteral v ::= n | true | f alse | null var x, y, i, o, t ::= ( a, j )

Figure 1 : Context Free Grammar for Concrete Execution serves as a compact representation of the conventional conditional (if-then-else) ex-pression. We use x, y, i, o, t to range over variables. Each variable is represented as a pair consisting of a name and a unique index, i.e., ( a, j ), where the index distinguishes different instances of the same variable across multiple executions of a code region. Furthermore, the IR models references through simple references z, which may either be concrete references r or reference variables x. Fields are denoted by f , classes by c, and method signatures by g.The statement language includes assignments, sequential composition (treated as an associative operator), the skip statement, and conditional statements of the form 

if , augmented with trailing ϕ-functions to capture the merging of variable values across branches. Method invocation is represented as invoke (x, z, g, −→y ), where x re-ceives the result of invoking method g on reference z with arguments −→y . The IR also includes return statements return e , field update statements put f ield (z, f, e ), which assign the value of expression e to field f of reference z, and field access statements 

get f ield (x, z, f ), which read the value of field f from reference z into variable x.Finally, object creation is represented by new (x, c ), where a new object of class c is allocated and assigned to reference variable x.Note that the full implementation of JR also supports array load and store oper-ations. However, since these can be modeled as specific instances of field operations, we omit their formal treatment here without loss of generality. 11 vals: V = Z ⊎ { true, f alse, null } ⊎ Rδ = (Θ , η, ∆, Hν, mu )method-stmt: Θ : C × G −→ method (−→var, stmt )ref-type: η : R −→ C

return-value: μ ∈ V ⊎ {⊥} 

Figure 2 : Environments and functions of Concrete Semantics 

## 4.2 Concrete Environment 

Fig. 2 presents the components of the concrete semantic environment δ =(Θ , η, ∆, H, ν, μ ). The environment captures the essential runtime state required for execution. In particular, Θ maps method statements to their corresponding signatures, while η maps reference identifiers to their associated types. The mapping ∆ represents the local variable environment, and H denotes the heap, which maps objects-fields pairs ( R × F ) to their associated values. The component ν is a counter used to gen-erate fresh object identifiers during allocation, and μ records return values produced during execution. Values in the semantic domain are drawn from the set Z of integers, the Boolean constants {true , false }, the distinguished value null , and the set of concrete references R.

## 4.3 Concrete Semantic Rules 

We present the evaluation rules of the concrete semantics in Appendix B1. The rules are standard operational semantics rules capturing the execution of statements in JR’s IR. In particular, the composition rule enforces sequential execution of statements 

s1 and s2. The corresponding composition-ret rule handles the case in which s1

evaluates to a return statement, in which case execution of s2 is ignored. The invoke rule define the semantics of method invocation. In this rule, the refer-ence expression z is first evaluated to a concrete reference r, which is then used together with the method signature g to retrieve the corresponding method body from Θ. A fresh environment is constructed by binding the formal parameters of the method to the evaluated argument values ( {(−→

y′ , −→

v′ )}), after which the method body is executed. Upon completion of the method body, the local environment ∆ is restored, and the return value is recorded in μ and assigned to the target variable x (∆[ x ← μ′]). Conditional execution is captured by the if-true , if-false , if-true-ret , and 

if-false-ret rules. In the non-returning cases ( if-true and if-false ), the condition expression e is evaluated to determine the branch to execute, followed by execution of the selected branch and the corresponding ϕ-statements. In the presence of a return 12 statement within the s1 branch ( if-true-ret and if-false-ret ), execution of the 

ϕ-statements is skipped, and the return value is propagated via μ′.The getfield and putfield statements follow the standard semantics of heap access. In both cases, the reference expression z is evaluated to a concrete reference 

r, after which the corresponding field f is either read from or updated in the heap H.Finally, object creation is handled by the new rule, which guarantees the generation of fresh references. Each new object is assigned a unique reference r produced by the freshRef function, which consumes the current counter value ν to ensure global freshness.

## 4.5 Concrete Evaluation Properties 

We assume the following properties hold over the concrete semantics 

• Statement must be defined in an SSA form (Property A.1). 

• Statement s always has at most a single return within an if-statement (Prop-erty A.2). 

• Each class has an class object reference which can be used to make static invocation (Property A.4). 

• All formal arguments of every method body in Θ are used in its statement (Property A.5). Next, we show the following lemma which are easy consequence of the rules of the concrete semantics and the SSA property (full details in [33]). 

Lemma 4.1. If for any reference r and classes c1, c 2, such that 

Θ ⊢ (( η, ∆, H, ν, μ ), s ) ⇒c (η′, ∆′, H ′, ν ′, μ ′)(r, c 1) ∈ η ∧ (r, c 2) ∈ η′

then c1 = c2.

13 to η is always guaranteed to have a unique reference, since its creation is parametric by ν,which gets incremented with each invocation of a new -statement. □

# 5 Dynamic Symbolic Execution’s Environment 

In this section, we present the formal foundation of Dynamic Symbolic Execution (DSE) upon which Java Ranger’s path-merging capabilities are built. We assume a sound symbolic execution that operates over a concrete state (Θ , η, ∆, H, ν, μ ) and a symbolic state (Θ , η s, ∆s, H s, π, μ s). The grammar of DSE follows the same structure as the concrete semantics in Fig. 1, with two key differences summarized in the unshaded variables in Fig. 5. 14 First, the value domain is extended from concrete values V to symbolic values Vs,allowing expressions to be used as values. Second, the environment is lifted accordingly. The method table Θ s and reference typing ηs are carried over unchanged from their concrete counterparts Θ and η respectively; they appear with symbolic subscripts solely to distinguish the symbolic environment from the concrete one. The local state ∆s maps uniquely indexed variables ( Id, n ) to values in Vs, and the heap Hs maps reference-field pairs to values in Vs, where references remain concrete while field values may be symbolic. Method return values μs range over Vs ⊎ {⊥} , where ⊥ denotes the absence of a return value. Finally, the path condition π captures the symbolic constraint accumulated along the current execution path. We make the following assumptions on the symbolic environment (ηs, ∆s, H s, π, μ s): 

• reference typing mapping is carried over unchanged from the concrete semantics, 

ηs = η (Property A.7). 

• No symbolic references (Property A.21). 

• The Heap and local variable mapping of the concrete state is consistent with the symbolic state (Properties A.10 and A.9). 

• All variables in original statement, that is going to be rewritten, are not indexed (Property A.19). 

• Initial local variable mapping ∆ s cannot have any use for any input variable i ∈ I

in the Range (∆ s) (Property A.11). 

# 6 Java Ranger’s Environment 

Java Ranger extends the environment of the DSE with the following environment variables: Path conditions are captured by the tuple Π = ( π, π r , π s), representing the current path condition, the return path condition, and a single-path condition, respectively. Region-specific variables are grouped in χ = ( I, O, T ), denoting input, output, and temporary variables. Finally, the environment includes auxiliary components that are initialized once: a global counter u ∈ N used for generating unique identifiers, and a field subscript mapping P , which associates reference-field pairs with symbolic terms to track field accesses. In addition to the DSE assumption, Java Ranger makes the following assumptions about the symbolic state before it starts the summarization:

## 6.2 Transformation Common Details 

Almost each transformation, except for the remove final-returns, has two statement judgments, one that is recursive, and another is a wrapper that initiate the transfor-mation process. Thus, in the proof we follow the same order, for statements, we first show the the soundness of the recursive judgment then discuss the soundness of the wrapper. In the remainder of this paper, we present the formal semantics and soundness proofs of main JR’s transformations. More precisely, we present eight transformations: 

γ transformation ( T R 1), early-returns elimination ( T R 2), final-return elimination (T R 3), renaming transformation ( T R 4), substitution transformation ( T R 5), method inlining transformation ( T R 6), field SSA transformation ( T R 7) and finally, constant reference propagation ( T R 8). 16 7 T R 1: γ-Creation Transformation

Figure 7 : Gamma Creation Rules Fig. 7 shows the rewrite rules for the γ-transformation. The rules define two judg-ments. The judgment φ ⊢ s −→ γ s′ rewrites an IR statement s into s′, where φ is a boolean flag encoding the local path condition within the statement, used to guide the rewriting of ϕ-expressions into γ-expressions. The wrapper judgment s −→ γ-wrap s′

is the triggering judgment that initiates the rewriting, invoking the γ-judgment on s

17 with an initial true path condition. Also, the noOp γ (s) is defined as all statements except for if and sequential composition statements. 

noOp γ (x := e) = true noOp γ (invoke (x, z, γ, −→y )) = true noOp γ (get f ield (x, z, f )) = true noOp γ (put f ield (z, f, e )) = true noOp γ (return e ) = true noOp γ (skip ) = true noOp γ (s) = f alse otherwise

# 8 T R 2: Early-Returns Elimination 

In this transformation, occurrences of multiple return statements are collapsed into one. In this transformation, occurrences of multiple return statements are collapsed into one. Fig. 8 shows the early-returns rules of the early-returns transformation. 19 The rules define the judgment s −→ ret (( πr , μ s), s ′), where s is the input state-ment, πr is a path condition indicating whether a return was reached, μs is the return value ( ⊥ if none), and s′ is the rewritten statement. The return-elimination trans-formation aims to eliminate early return statements by collapsing all early-return statements into a single return at the end of the rewritten statement. To ensure a sound transformation, two pieces of information must be captured: the path condi-tion πr under which a return was reached, and the returned expression μs. These are propagated by subsequent transformations to preserve the semantics of early returns. The transformation is triggered by the wrapper judgment s −→ ret -wrap (πr , ·), which has two cases depending on whether a return was eliminated in s or not. Rule ret-wrap applies when a return was found (μs̸ = ⊥ and πr̸ = f alse ), appending a conditional if πr return μs to the rewritten statement s′. Ob-serve at s′ have no return-statements, and that the subsequent if-statement in (s′; if πr then return μs else skip ) adds a single return-statement guarded by a summarized condition (πr) and a summarized returned expression (μs). Rule ret-wrap-none applies when no return was found, leaving the statement unchanged. Rule ret-return is the base case, handling a return e statement by setting the return path condition πr = true and the return expression μs = e, replacing the statement with skip .For compound control flow, the transformation must collect return information from all reachable sub-statements. Rule ret-if handles conditionals by processing both branches independently and merging their return conditions and expressions using the γ expression. Rule ret-comp similarly processes both s1 and s2 in a sequential composition, collecting return information from each side. In sequential composition, a return may occur in either s1 or s2. The merged re-turn condition π∗ 

> r

is simp (γ(πr , true, π ′

> r

)): if s1 returned then π∗ 

> r

is true , otherwise it takes the return condition π′ 

> r

collected from rewriting s2. The merged return expres-sion μ∗ 

> s

follows the same spirit: it is conditioned on πr, taking μs when s1 returned, and otherwise deferring to μ′ 

> s

when s2 returned, defaulting to f alse when neither did. The residual guards s′ 

> 2

with ! πr to ensure it executes only if s1 did not return. Finally, the ret-no-op rule covers other types of statements that the transformation does not change/rewrite. The noOp ret (s) is defined as 

noOp ret (x := e) = true noOp ret (invoke (x, z, γ, −→y )) = true noOp ret (get f ield (x, z, f )) = true noOp ret (put f ield (z, f, e )) = true noOp ret (skip ) = true noOp ret (s) = f alse otherwise The transformation also defines the simp function which simplifies γ expressions by collapsing degenerate cases: it returns ⊥ when both branches are ⊥, and f alse when both branches are f alse , avoiding unnecessary symbolic branching in the collected return environment. 20 8.1 Early Returns Elimination is Sound

Theorem 8.2. Let s be a statement. If 

Θ ⊢ (( η, ∆, H, μ ), s ) ⇒c (η′, ∆′, H ′, μ ′)

s −→ ret −wrap (πr , s ′)Θ ⊢ (( η, ∆, H, μ ), s ′) ⇒c (η′′ , ∆′′ , H ′′ , μ ′′ )

then 

η′′ = η′, ∆′′ = ∆ ′, H ′′ = H′, ν ′′ = ν′, μ ′′ = μ′

## 8.2 Properties 

The early-return elimination transformation assumes all the guarantees by the γ-creation transformation, and it provides the following guarantees. Given s −→ ret −wrap (πr, ˆs), this transformation guarantees the following properties 23 • Rewritten Statement ˆ s satisfies the SSA (property A.1).   

> Proof This is clear since none of the rules in Fig. 8 modify assignments within generalized assignment statements, update local/heap mappings, or introduce new variables. □

• Rewritten statement always has at most a single return within an if-statement (Property A.13). 

• If the original statement s has a return statement, then there must be a valid return-condition, and the rewritten statement must be of a specific form (Property A.14). 

• Return condition ( πr) is true on all return paths (Property A.15).    

> Proof This can be directly be shown by applying the recursive theorem of the ret judgment.
> □

# 9 T R 3: Remove Final Return 

integer n ∈ Z subscript j ∈ Z+ ⊎ {⊥} identif ier a, b, c ∈ Id class c ∈ C signature g ∈ G f ield f ∈ F ref erence r ∈ Rliteral v ::= n | true | f alse | null var x, y, i, o, t ::= ( a, j )

binaryOp ⋄ ::= + | − | ∗ | ÷ | & | bitwise − or | ⊕ | % |== |! = |≤|≥| && | logical -or |> |< |≪|≫ 

exp e ::= v | x | e1 ⋄ e2 | e | γ(e, e 1, e 2)

simple -ref. z ::= r | xstmt s ::= s1; s2 | x := e | skip | if e then s 1 else s 2 | invoke (x, z, g, −→y ) | return e put f ield (z, f, e ) | get f ield (x, z, f ) | new (x, c )

Figure 9 : Remove Final Return to-grammar The early-return elimination transformation does three things: it captures the return condition πr and the return expression μs into the environment, and appends a sin-gle return statement guarded by πr of the form if π r then return μ s else skip ,replacing all early returns. The final-return elimination transformation then removes this guarded return statement, as πr and μs in the environment already cap-ture sufficient information about the return for subsequent transformations. Fig. 10 shows the two rules of this transformation. Rule final-noop handles the case where πr = f alse , indicating that no early return was eliminated, and thus no guarded return is present; the statement is left unchanged and μs is set to ⊥.Rule final-ret handles the case where πr̸ = f alse , using pattern matching to identify 24 the guarded if π r then return e else skip appended by the early-return elimination transformation, stripping it from the statement and recording μs = e.

## 9.1 Remove Final Return Transformation is Sound 

Let us define evalReturn (∆ , π r, μ s) ≜

(

v if (∆ , π r) ⇝c true ∧ (∆ , μ s) ⇝c v

⊥ otherwise 

Lemma 9.1 (evalReturn is not changed by extension of the environment) .

evalReturn (∆ , π r, μ s) = evalReturn (∆ ′, π r, μ s)

where ∆ ⊆ ∆′

Proof □

Corollary 9.2 (Remove final-return transformation is sound) . If for any statement 

s and a rewriting state (Θ t, η s, ∆s, H s, μ s, (π, π r , π s), (I, O, T ), u ),

Θ ⊢ (( η, ∆, H, ν, μ ), s ) ⇒c (η′, ∆′, H ′, ν ′, μ ′)

s −→ remove −ret (μs, s ′)Θ ⊢ (( η, ∆, H, ν, μ ), s ′) ⇒c (η′′ , ∆′′ , H ′′ , ν ′′ , μ ′′ )

then 

η′′ = η′, ∆′′ = ∆ ′, H ′′ = H′, ν ′′ = ν′, μ ′′ = ⊥

μ′ = evalReturn (∆ ′′ , π r , μ s)

Proof Since there are only two rewrite rules that can apply, we will proceed with our proof by considering the two possible cases for πr : when it is the formula f alse , and when it is not. First, consider the case when πr is f alse . According to assumed early-return prop-erty(Property A.15), μ′ must be ⊥, which also means that s contains no return statements. Otherwise, if a control flow existed with μ′̸ = ⊥ but πr = f alse , this would violate the early-return (Property A.15). Thus, s cannot be of the form s′′ ; if π r then return e else skip ,and the final-noop rule applies without modifying s. Hence, s′ = s and μ′ = ⊥. Conse-quently, executing s′ yields the same state, i.e., η′′ = η′, ∆ ′′ = ∆ ′, H′′ = H′, ν′′ = ν′, and 

μ′′ = μ = ⊥ (conclusion 2). Finally, in this case, the second branch of evalReturn applies, giving evalReturn (∆ ′′ , π r , μ ′

> s

) = ⊥ = μ′ (conclusion 3). The second case is when πr̸ = f alse . From the assumed property of early-returns transformation (Property A.14), s must be of the form 

s = s′′ ; if π r then return e else skip 

Executing s concretely first applies composition , evaluating s′′ as Θ ⊢ (( η, ∆, H, ν, μ ), s ′′ ) ⇒c

(η′′′ , ∆′′′ , H ′′′ , ν ′′′ , μ ′′′ ), where μ′′′ = ⊥, followed by evaluating the conditional using either 

if-phi-true or if-phi-false , depending on πr .If πr evaluates to true , we apply if-phi-true (since ¬hasReturn (s′′ ), if-phi-true-ret 

cannot apply), yielding Θ ⊢ (( η′′′ , ∆′′′ , H ′′′ , ν ′′′ , μ ′′′ = ⊥), if π r then return e else skip ) ⇒c (η′′′ , ∆′′′ , H ′′′ , ν ′′′ , μ 4)

25 where η′ = η′′′ , ∆ ′ = ∆ ′′′ , H′ = H′′′ , ν′ = ν′′′ , and μ′ = μ4 = v, with ∆ ′′′ ⊢ e ⇝c v.Since πr̸ = f alse and s = s′′ ; if πr then return e else skip , the final-ret rule applies, giving s′ = s′′ and μs = e. Executing s′ gives Θ ⊢ (( η, ∆, H, ν, μ ), s ′′ ) ⇒c

(η′′′ , ∆′′′ , H ′′′ , ν ′′′ , μ ′′′ ), with η′′ = η′′′ = η′, ∆ ′′ = ∆′′′ = ∆′, H′′ = H′′′ = H′,

ν′′ = ν′′′ = ν′, and μ′′′ = μ = ⊥ (conclusion 2). Because πr̸ = f alse , the first condition of evalReturn applies. Since ∆ ′′ = ∆ ′′′ , we have ∆ ′′′ ⊢ e ⇝c v1 with v1 = v = μ4 = μ

(conclusion 3). If, instead, πr evaluates to f alse , if-phi-false applies, executing skip and yielding Θ ⊢ (( η′′′ , ∆′′′ , H ′′′ , ν ′′′ , μ ′′′ = ⊥), if π r then return e else skip ) ⇒c (η′′′ , ∆′′′ , H ′′′ , ν ′′′ , μ 4)such that η′ = η′′′ , ∆ ′ = ∆ ′′′ , H′ = H′′′ , ν′ = ν′′′ , and μ′′′ = μ4 = ⊥ = μ′, with ∆ ′′′ ⊢

## 9.2 Properties of the Final-Returns Transformation 

The remove final-return transformation assumes all properties guaranteed by the early-return elimination transformation, and it provide the following guarantees. Given πr ⊢

s −→ f inal −wrap (μs, s ′), we have that 

• Rewritten statement s′ satisfies the SSA property (Property A.1). More formally, 

SSA (s′) holds. 

Proof This is straightforward: since s satisfies the SSA property and none of the rules in Fig. 10 introduce new variables or update existing ones, it follows that isSSA (s′). □

• Statement s′ has no return statements (Property A.16). More formally, 

¬hasReturn (s′) is valid. 

Proof We know that the only case where a return statement can exist in s is when it is of the from s = ˆ s; if π r then return e else skip , and that πr̸ = f alse (Property A.14). This means that ˆ s cannot contain another return statement because atMostSingleIfReturn(s) 

would be violated. Therefore, when we can only apply final-ret rule, which removes the enclosed return statement on the then-side, and rewrites the statement s to ˆ s, where ˆ s = s′,which we have just shown that it cannot contain a return statement, i.e., ¬hasReturn (s′). 

□

# 10 T R 4: Renaming Transformation 

This transformation assigns a unique index to every local variable in the statement 

s. Specifically, each variable of the form ( a, ⊥) is renamed to ( a, j ), where j ∈ Z+,ensuring that distinct occurrences of the same variable across multiple summarizations of s, i.e., within loops or recursive calls, are uniquely distinguished. Fig. 11 shows a snippet of the main renaming rules. The rules define two judgments. The judgment u ⊢ s −→ rename s′ rewrites an IR statement s into s′, where u is the global unique index used to assign fresh version numbers to unversioned variables. The wrapper judgment I, O ⊢ (( πr, μ s, T, u ), s ) −→ rename -wrap (( μ′

> s

, π ′

> r

, T ′, u ′), s ′) is 26 the entry point of the transformation, triggering the rename judgment on s. Here, I

and O are the sets of input and output variables, πr and μs are the early-return path condition and symbolic return expression, and T is the set of temporary variables. Their primed counterparts π′ 

> r

, μ′

> s

, T ′, and u′ reflect their updated values after the rewriting. For example, rule rename-var renames a variable x to x′ by applying unique u(x), which assigns the current counter u as the version index if x has not yet been renamed, i.e., if it is of the form ( a, ⊥). If x already carries a non-⊥ index, unique u leaves it unchanged, as captured by the side condition j̸ = ⊥ in the definition of unique u at the top of Fig. 11. This behavior is deliberate: it allows e 7 →rename e′ to be applied over partially renamed statements, skipping variables that have already been versioned and acting only on those that remain unversioned, i.e., those carrying ⊥.Rule rename-unaryOp propagates the renaming into the operand e, deriving u ⊢

e 7 →rename e′ in the premise, and reconstructs e′ around the renamed result. The rule does not itself rename anything directly but serves as a structural rule that drives the renaming relation into sub-expressions. Rules for other expression forms, including binary operators and γ-expressions, are omitted from Fig. 11 for brevity, but follow the same pattern of propagating renaming onto their constituent sub-expressions. The renaming transformation is propagated structurally over statements. Rule rename-assign handles assignment by renaming both the right-hand side ex-pression e and the left-hand side variable x independently, producing the renamed assignment x′ := e′. Rule rename-comp handles sequential composition by propagating the renaming relation into each sub-statement independently under the same counter 

u, yielding s′

> 1

; s′

> 2

.Other statement forms follow the same pattern. Rules for conditionals, 

rename-putfield , and rename-invoke , etc., all propagate renaming into their sub-statements and invoke renaming on their composing expressions. Notably, 

rename-invoke renames the variables and expressions appearing in the call site with-out inlining the body of the invoked method; the inlining of method bodies is deferred to a dedicated later transformation. Finally, the rule rename-wrap extends the renaming transformation to environment variables that live outside the statement but must be consistently renamed to match their new names within it ( πr and μs are renamed to π′ 

> r

and μ′

> s

). Using the same counter u′ = u + 1, it renames the input/output sets I, O, and the temporary set 

T via unique u′ , and applies u′ ⊢ s −→ rename s′ to the statement, ensuring all unversioned variables across both the environment and the statement receive the same index. To preserve the external interface of s, the rewritten statement s′′ wraps s′ with assignments that bind the old input names to their renamed counterparts before s′,and bind the renamed output variables back to the old output names after, ensuring 

s′′ exposes the same input/output interface as s.

## 10.1 Renaming Transformation is Sound 

To show the soundness of this transformation, we first show the soundness of the expression judgment, and follow that up with our usual discussion about the soundness of the statement recursive judgment and then the wrapper judgment. 27 Lemma 10.1 (Expression Renaming is Sound) . Let unique u(∆) =

{(unique u(x), ∆( x)) | x ∈ Dom (∆) }, then for any concrete expression e and for any integer u, if 

∆ ⊢ e ⇝c v ∧ u ⊢ e 7 →rename e′

then, we have 

unique u(∆) ⊢ e′ ⇝c v

That is, evaluating the rewritten expression will result to the same value v that is obtained when evaluating the original expression, if and only if the evaluation used the local variable mapping from unique u(∆). 

Proof We will proceed by induction on the judgment u ⊢ e 7 →rename e′.First, observe that the lemma proves trivially on rename-val rule, since there are no variables contained within values, and thus no rewriting is done. Now, consider the rename-var 

rule, we have, u ⊢ x 7 →rename x′, where e′ = x′ = unique u(x) = ( x, u ). Applying the var 

rule in the concrete semantics, we obtain ∆ ⊢ x ⇝c v, such that ∆( x) = v. Now evaluating the rewritten expression ( unique u(∆))( x, u ) = v, directly from the definition of the unique 

function, thus, we have unique u(∆) ⊢ (x, u ) ⇝c v.Consider the rename-binaryOp rule. the rewriting will produce u ⊢ e1 ⋄e2 7 →rename e′ 

> 1

⋄e′

> 2

,where u ⊢ e1 7 →rename e′

> 1

, u ⊢ e2 7 →rename e′

> 2

Now, we define the lemma for statements, and then discuss its proof.

> 1

, for some u,where x′ = unique u(x) and u ⊢ e 7 →rename e′

evaluating to the same value v when evaluated on the unique local mapping, i.e., unique u(∆) ⊢ e′

⇝c v.Now, evaluating the rewritten statement on the unique local mapping, we have Θ ⊢

(( η, unique u(∆) , H, ν, μ ), x ′ := e′) ⇒c (η, ∆′′ , H, ν, μ ), where ∆ ′′ = unique u(∆)[ x′ ← v], and x′ = unique u(x) = ( x, u ). Now observe that, ∆ ′′ = unique u(∆)[ x′ ← v] = unique u(∆ ′). This observation follows directly from applying the unique u function on ∆ ′, and from observing that ∆ ′(x) = unique u(∆)( x′). Next, consider the rename-comp rule. Applying the rewriting we get u ⊢ s1; s2 −→ rename

. There are two cases for executing a composition statement in the concrete semantics, either s1

has a return control flow ( composition-ret rule), or not ( composition rule). But, recall by from the previous transformation (remove final-returns), it is guaranteed that return 

statements are guaranteed to not exist in s A.16 that also enforced by the grammar of the renaming transformation. This assumption is safe to assume because the renaming transformation always happens after the remove final-return transformation, which ensures that all return statements are removed from s, while capturing the semantics and the conditions of the returns within the rewriting environment variables μs, and πr . There-fore, since s1 cannot contain a return statement, we can apply the composition rule from the concrete semantics to get Θ ⊢ (( η, ∆, H, ν, μ ), s 1) ⇒c (η1, ∆1, H 1, ν 1, μ ), and Θ ⊢ (( η1, ∆1, H 1, ν 1, μ ), s 2) ⇒c (η2, ∆2, H 2, ν 2, μ ). > 1

unique u(∆)[ unique u(x) ← v]. That is, updating the mapping of the variable x, then renam-ing it using the unique u function, is the same as, renaming the variable then updating its mapping. To see how this is possible, let ( x, v ′) be the mapping of x in ∆. Then, ∆[ x ← v], 

29 will result in updating the ( x, v ′) mapping with ( x, v ) mapping. Now, applying unique u, i.e.,

## 10.2 Properties of the Renaming Transformation 

The renaming transformation assumes all properties guaranteed by earlier transforma-tions ( γ creation, early-returns elimination and remove final-returns), and it provides the following guaranteed properties. 32 For any statement s with I, O, T representing the statement input, output, and temporary variables, and u representing unique-count respectively, if ( x, n ) ∈ (Id, n )is a variable within s′, such that 

I, O ⊢ (( πr, μ s, T, u ), s ) −→ rename −wrap (μ′

> s

, π ′

> r

, T ′′ , u ′), s ′′ )where u′ = u + 1 , χ = ( I, O, T ), χ ′ = ( I′, O ′, T ′), χ ′ = unique u′ (χ), I = {i1..i n}, O =

{o1..o m}

, u ′ ⊢ s −→ rename s′, s ′′ = ( i1, u ′) := i1; .. ; ( in, u ′) := in; s′; o1 := ( o1, u ′); .. ; om := (om, u ′); 

u′ ⊢ μs 7 →rename μ′

> s

, u ′ ⊢ πr 7 →rename π′ 

> r

, T ′′ = ( I′ ∪ O′ ∪ T ′)then we have 

• Rewritten Statement s′′ satisfies the SSA property (Property A.1). More formally, 

SSN (s′′ ). 

Proof Let s′′ = ( i1, u ′) := i1; · · · ; ( in, u ′) := in; s′; o1 := ( o1, u ′); · · · ; om := ( om, u ′). To show that SSA (s′′ ) holds, note first that by the SSA assumed property from the previous transformation (guaranteed property from remove final-returns transformation, Property A.1) we have SSA (s), and since s′ is obtained from s by a systematic renam-ing, SSA (s′) follows. Next, the prefix assignments ( ik, u ′) := ik introduce only fresh variables ( ik, u ′), and since ik ∈ I implies ¬isDef (ik, s ) ∧ isU se (ik, s ), it follows that 

¬isDef (( ik, u ′), s ′′ ) ∧ isU se (( ik, u ′), s ′′ ), hence no clashes arise with s′. Finally, the suf-fix assignments oj := ( oj , u ′) do not introduce new definitions, because all occurrences of 

oj have already been renamed to ( oj , u ′) in s′, and thus they preserve SSA. Therefore, 

SSA (s′′ ) holds. For the detailed proof, please refer to our technical report [33]. □

• Fresh variables have u + 1 suffices (Property A.17). More formally, ( x, ⊥) is in s,then all its occurrences with s′ must have been renamed to ( x, u + 1). 

Proof This is easy to show as by JR assumptions, we know that all variables in s must have a ⊥ index (Property A.19), for example, for an identifier x, its corresponding variable form that appear in s must be ( x, ⊥). Next, observe that renaming of variables are done 

rename-var , which renames all variables to have an index u + 1, i.e., ( x, u + 1) from our continued example above. Observe that happens over all expressions in s, following the rules in Fig. 11. # 11 T R 5: Input Substitution Transformation 

The substitution transformation substitutes values of the region’s inputs that are de-fined in I. Fig. 12, shows a snippet of the rules for the substitution transformation. The rules define two judgments. The judgment ∆ s, I ⊢ s −→ sub s′ rewrites an IR statement s into s′ by substituting variables with their corresponding values in the symbolic local variable mapping ∆ s, where I is the set of input variables whose occur-rences drive the substitution. The wrapper judgment ∆ s ⊢ (( πr, μ s, I ), s ) −→ sub -wrap 

(( π′ 

> r

, μ ′

> s

, I ′), s ′) is the entry point of the transformation, triggering the sub judgment on s and additionally applying the substitution to the early-return path condition πr

and symbolic return expression μs, producing their updated counterparts π′ 

> r

and μ′

> s

,and updating the input set from I to I′.The substitution transformation replaces variables with their corresponding values in ∆ s. For expressions, Rule sub-expr directly substitutes each variable occurrence with its mapped value in ∆ s. For statements, the transformation propagates substitu-tion over all uses of variables, leaving definitions unchanged. Rule sub-wrap extends the substitution to environment variables that live outside the statement, applying it to the path condition π, the return condition πr, and the return expression μs.Additionally, the wrapper updates the input set I to reflect the free variables in all rewritten expressions, as the previous input variables in I have been replaced by their mapped values in ∆ s and are no longer needed as inputs. 

Lemma 11.1 (Expression Substitution is Sound) . For any concrete expression e, if 

34 ∆ ⊢ e ⇝c v

∆s, I ⊢ e 7 →sub e′

∀i ∈ I. ¬isU se (i, e ′)

Proof We will proceed by induction on the judgment ∆ s, I ⊢ e 7 →sub e′. For this proof, let ∆ , H represents the concrete variable mapping and the concrete heap, while ∆ s, H s

and π represents the symbolic variable mapping, the symbolic heap and the path condi-tion, respectively. Finally, I represents the input variables. Also, we have that (∆ ′, H ) |=(∆ s, H s, π ). First, consider sub-val . Since the rewrite does not modify the value v, and v always evaluates to itself, we trivially have ∆ ′ ⊢ v ⇝c v. Moreover, v does not perform any lookup in ∆ during evaluation, and ∀i ∈ I. ¬isU se (i, v ) holds immediately, as v is a val. Next, consider sub-var-triv where e = x and x / ∈ I. In this case, no rewriting occurs, i.e., e = x = e′. Now, observe that since ∆ ⊆ ∆′ and the fact that (∆ ′) |= (∆ s, π ), then evaluating x in ∆ ′ must yield the same value as the value resulting from evaluating it in ∆, i.e., ∆ ⊢ e′ ⇝c v and ∆ ′ ⊢ e′ ⇝c v (conclusion 1). The second conclusion holds trivially, since x is not an input. Now, consider sub-var , where e = x and x ∈ I, yielding ∆ s, I ⊢ x 7 →sub e′ with 

e′ = ∆ s(x). The first conclusion holds trivially from ∆ ⊆ ∆′ and the fact that (∆ ′) |= (∆ s, π ). The second conclusion holds since we have substituted the value ∆ s(x) for x and we have that ∀i ∈ I. ∀x ∈ Dom (∆ s).¬isU se (i, ∆s(x)), then we can conclude that e′ = ∆ s(x) does not use any input variable. Theorem 11.2 (Statement Recursive Substitution is Sound) . Let s be any statement. If 

Θ ⊢ (( η, ∆, H, ν, μ ), s ) ⇒c (ηs, ∆′, H ′, ν ′, μ ′)∆s, I ⊢ s −→ sub s′

∆′ ⊆ ∆′′′ 

∀i ∈ I. ¬isU se (i, s ′)

Proof Let ηs, ∆, H, ν, μ, ∆s, H s, π, I, O, ∆, ∆′′ and s satisfy the premises of the theorem. We will prove this theorem by using structural induction. There-fore, we will have that ∆ ′′′ = ∆ ′′ and ∆ ′ = ∆, and thus, the first and second conclusion hold, trivially. The third conclusion also holds because a skip by definition cannot contain an input variable. In the sub-new case, the statement s is unchanged (s′ = s). Thus, we have that (η′, ∆′, H ′, ν ′, μ ′) be the state from evaluating the original statement, Θ ⊢

(( η, ∆, H, ν, μ ), new (x, c )) ⇒c (η′, ∆′, H ′, ν ′, μ ′), such that ν′ = ν + 1 , ∆′ = ∆[ x ← r], η ′ =

Corollary 11.3 (The substitution-wrapper is sound) . If 

Θ ⊢ (( η, ∆, H, ν, μ ), s ) ⇒c (η′, ∆′, H ′, ν ′, μ ′)∆s ⊢ (( πr, μ s, I ), s ) −→ sub −wrap (( π′

The substitution assumes all guaranteed properties from γ-creation, remove final-returns and renaming transformations. And it guarantees the following properties. Given ∆ s ⊢ (( πr, μ s, I ), s ) −→ sub −wrap (( π′

• Rewritten Statement s′ satisfies the generalized SSA property. More formally, 

SSA (s′) holds. 

Proof This follows directly, from renaming transformation guarantee (Property A.1) that the SSA (s) holds and that none of the expression rules ( 7→ sub ) alter the local variable mapping or the heap mappings. Likewise, none of the statement rules ( →sub ) modify the variables assigned within generalized assignment statements, nor do they introduce new variables. Consequently, because neither variable mappings nor assignments are changed, the generalized SSA property is preserved for s′, i.e., SSA (s′) holds. □

• No input variables i ∈ I exist in the rewritten statement s′. More formally, ∀x ∈

I, ¬isU se (x, s ′)

Proof This follows directly from applying the soundness theorem 11.4 of the substitution transformation. □

# 12 T R 6: Method Inlining Transformation 

This transformation inlines the IR of called methods one level at a time. Inlining is performed only when the object reference on which the method is invoked is a concrete reference. Fig. 13 shows a snippet of the main rules for this transformation. The rules define two judgments. The judgment Θ t, η s, ∆s ⊢ (( T, u ), s ) −→ inline (( T ′, u ′), s ′) rewrites an IR statement s into s′ by inlining method calls, where Θ t is the mapping of trans-formed method bodies, ηs is the reference-to-type mapping, ∆ s is the symbolic local variable mapping, T is the set of temporary variables, and u is the global unique in-dex. Their primed counterparts T ′ and u′ reflect their updated values after rewriting. The wrapper judgment Θ t, η s, ∆s ⊢ (( T, u ), s ) −→ inline -wrap (( T ′, u ′), s ′) is the entry point of the transformation, triggering the inline judgment on s.Rule inline-invoke inlines a method call by looking up its transformed body in Θt, which contains the IR of methods on which the γ-transformation, early-return elimination, and final-return removal have already been applied (see Appendix B). The rule retrieves the transformed body, applies the renaming transformation to ensure uniqueness of variable names, and applies substitution to propagate the passed argu-ments into the body. The variable x is then assigned the modified return expression 

μ3 

> s

of the inlined method. Rules inline-comp propagates the transformation into their sub-statements, threading the temporary variable set T and counter u through each; the rule for conditionals, omitted from Fig. 13 for brevity, follows the same pattern. 41 All remaining statement forms, including assignments, getfield, and putfield, etc., are handled by Rule inline-no-op , which leaves them unchanged.

## 12.2 Method Inlining Properties 

This transformation assumes all the guaranteed properties from pervious transfor-mations, γ-creation, remove final-returns, renaming and substitution transformations. In addition, it also assumes this property which is enforced by ensuring that the method transformation must have proceeded the method-inlining transformation (Property A.20). The method-transform is a static transformation that is done on all methods to populate Θ t with the corresponding method statements, after applying 

γ-creation, early-returns elimination and remove final-returns transformations. Refer to section B for more detail about this static transformation. Now assuming we have that Θt, η s, ∆s ⊢ (( T, u ), s ) −→ inline −wrap (( T ′′ , u ′), s ′)such that ∆′

# 13 T R 7: Field SSA Transformation 

In this transformation, field statements ( getfield , putfield ) are eliminated and re-placed by a sequence of assignment statements that satisfies the SSA property. Fig. 14 shows the rules for the field transformation. The rules define two judgments. The judgment ∆ s, H s ⊢ (( T, P, ⊥), s ) −→ f ield (( T ′, P ′, p ), s ′) rewrites an IR statement 

s into s′, where Hs is the symbolic heap, P is the path-subscript map tracking the latest update to each reference-field pair along the current local path in s, and p

is the local path condition guarding reference-field updates. Initially, P agrees with 

Hs and p = ⊥. The updated map P ′ and flag p′ reflect the state of field ac-cesses after rewriting. The wrapper judgment ∆ s, H s ⊢ (( I, O, T ), s ) −→ f ield -wrap 

(( I, O, T ′′ ), (addGets (P ); skip ; s′; skip ; addPuts (P ′))) is the entry point of the trans-formation, initializing P from Hs and triggering the field judgment on s, then wrapping the result with addGets (P ) and addPuts (P ′) to load and store field values at the boundaries of the rewritten region. More precisely, the rule field-wrap is the entry point for the field transformation. It initializes the field map P via initialP (Hs), which assigns a fresh temporary vari-able t to each field-reference pair ( r, f ) in the domain of Hs, representing the latest SSA variable carrying the value of that field along the current path. These fresh tem-poraries are added to T to produce T ′, and the field transformation is then applied to 

s under T ′ and P , yielding a rewritten statement s′, an updated field map P ′, and a path condition p. The rule wraps s′ with addGets (P ) before and addPuts (P ′) after, which emit the necessary getfield and putfield instructions to load field values into their corresponding temporaries before execution and write them back after, respec-tively. The output temporary set T ′′ reflects all temporaries introduced during the transformation. The wrapping of s′ with addGets (P ) and addPuts (P ′) serves an important pur-pose beyond a single pass. Since field references are not always resolved to concrete references in one iteration, the transformation may require multiple fixpoint iterations until all reference variables are concretized. The wrapping captures the progress made in the current iteration: addGets (P ) records the getfield eliminations already per-formed, and addPuts (P ′) records the putfield eliminations, so that if a later iteration resolves a previously unresolved reference to a concrete one, the transformation can resume from where it left off rather than restarting from scratch. The skip statements 49 surrounding s′ in the residual serve as delimiters for pattern matching: in subsequent iterations, the field transformation is applied only to s′ and not to the entire residual statement. This is essential to avoid an infinite loop, as re-applying the transforma-tion to addGets (P ) or addPuts (P ′) would cause their instructions to be treated as new field accesses requiring further elimination. The ⊥ flag in the field judgment (( T ′, P, ⊥), s ) −→ f ield (( T ′′ , P ′, p ), s ′) acts as a control flag that governs whether field elimination should continue. As long as the flag is ⊥, the transformation proceeds, eliminating getfield and putfield state-ments and updating P accordingly. However, upon encountering a method invocation, Rule field-invoke immediately flips the flag to ⊤ and halts further field elimination, flushing the current field map by prepending addPuts (P ) before the invoke statement. This is necessary because inlining the invoked method may introduce new concrete references that affect the SSA assignments of subsequent statements, and proceeding with elimination before those references are resolved would violate the sequentializa-tion of SSA. Once the flag is set to ⊤, it propagates through compound statements: Rule field-if-2, field-if-3, field-if-4 and the corresponding sequential com-position rule (not shown) propagate ⊤ to prevent any further elimination in the remainder of the statement, ensuring that field elimination resumes only in the next fixpoint iteration after inlining has been applied. The core of the field transformation lies in the elimination of getfield and 

putfield statements, replacing them with simple variable assignments using the field map P .Rule field-put eliminates a putfield instruction by introducing a fresh temporary variable t and updating the field map to P ′ = P [( r, f ) ← t], recording that t now carries the latest value of the field-reference pair ( r, f ). The putfield is replaced by the assignment t := e. Rule field-get eliminates a getfield instruction by looking up the latest temporary t associated with ( r, f ) in P and replacing the getfield with the assignment x := t, leaving P unchanged. Both rules require that r is a concrete reference, i.e., r ∈ R, if references has been resolved to their concrete values, otherwise, no rewriting is performed ( field-put-var and field-get-var rules, see our technical report [33] for complete set of rules). Finally, the rule field-if-1 handles the case where field elimination succeeds completely in both branches of a conditional, i.e., neither branch encounters a method invocation. Each branch is processed independently under the same initial field map P ,yielding rewritten statements s′ 

> 1

and s′ 

> 2

and updated field maps P ′ and P ′′ respectively. Since the two branches may assign different temporaries to the same field-reference pair (r, f ), the maps P ′ and P ′′ must be reconciled after the conditional. This is handled by the merge function, which compares P ′ and P ′′ and, for each field-reference pair (r, f ) where the two branches disagree, introduces a fresh temporary t assigned to 

γ(e, t 1, t 2), where t1 = P ′(r, f ) and t2 = P ′′ (r, f ) are the temporaries from the left and right branches respectively. The merge proceeds recursively until all disagreements are resolved, producing a unified field map ˆP and a sequence of γ-assignments ˆ s appended after the conditional. If both branches produce identical field maps, merge returns 

skip and leaves P unchanged. 50 13.1 Field Transformation is Sound 

In this section, we discuss the soundness of the Field Transformation. We first present a consistency properties over the path-subscript map, then we show the soundness of the recursive judgment followed by the soundness of the wrapper judgment. For any path-subscript map P , local variable mapping ∆, and a Heap , we define a 

P -Heap consistency relation ( P, H ′, ∆′) ⋊ H′ for all reference and field pairs ( r, f ), as (P, H ′, ∆′) ⋊ H iff ∀(r, f ) ∈ Dom (H).H (r, f ) = 

(

∆′(P (r, f )) if ( r, f ) ∈ Dom( P )

H′(r, f ) otherwise Intuitively, the relation ( P, H ′, ∆′) ⋊ H defines when a concrete heap H is consistent with a symbolic field map P , a base heap H′, and a local variable mapping ∆ ′.Specifically, for each field-reference pair ( r, f ), H must agree with the value of the corresponding temporary variable P (r, f ) in ∆ ′ if ( r, f ) is tracked in P , and fall back to the base heap H′ otherwise. A key property of the merge function is that it preserves the P-Heap consistency relation across both branches of a conditional. Intuitively, if both branches indepen-dently maintain P-Heap consistency with their respective field maps P1 and P2, then the γ-assignments produced by merge yield a unified field map ˆP that remains consis-tent with the concrete heap H, regardless of which branch was taken. This is formalized in Lemma 13.1.

Before establishing the soundness of the field transformation, we state two prelim-inary results. Corollary 13.2 establishes a domain invariant on the field map P : after 52 any application of the field judgment, either the flag is set to ⊤ and P ′ is empty, re-flecting that field elimination was halted by a method invocation, or the flag remains 

⊥ and the domain of P ′ is preserved and equals that of Hs. This ensures that P always tracks exactly the set of field-reference pairs in Hs throughout the transformation. Lemma 13.3 establishes that the field judgment preserves the generalized SSA prop-erty: if s satisfies SSA (s) before the transformation, then the rewritten statement s′

satisfies SSA (s′) after. These two results together provide the necessary foundations for the soundness argument of the field transformation. 

Corollary 13.2. [Either P is empty, or its domain is the same to the domain of Hs]If 

∆s, H s ⊢ (( T, P, p ), s ) −→ f ield (( T ′, P ′, p ′), s ′)

then 

p′ = ⊤ ∧ P ′ = ∅

or 

p′ = ⊥ ∧ Dom (P ) = Dom (Hs) = Dom (P ′)

Proof It is easy to see why this theorem is true. Initially, the field-wrap rule triggers the 

←f ield judgment, which sets up P to have the same domain as Hs. Next, field-no-op ,

field-get-var , field-put-var , field-invoke , field-if-2 , field-if-3 , and field-if-4 rules, all end up with p′ = ⊥, but we can also see that in these cases P ′ = ∅.For field-get , we can clearly see that P ′ is the same as P , while p′ = ⊥, so the remark holds here too. The rules that introduce a change on P ′ that is different from P includes put-field ,

field-comp and field-if-1 . For put-field , we P ′ is created from P as P ′ = P [( r, f ) ← t], where r, f are a reference, and field pair, while t is a fresh temporary variable. But since 

r, f already exists in P , then P ′ is actually updating the mapping of an existing r, f pair, rather than introducing a new entry. Thus in this case we have that Dom (P ) = Dom (Hs) = 

Dom (P ′). In the field-comp rule, the theorem hold straightforward by using induction over the judgments used to transform s1, and s2.Finally, for field-if-1 rule, we have that ∆s, H s ⊢

(( T, P, p ), if e then s 1 else s 2) −→ f ield (( T2, ˆP , ˆp), if e then s ′ 

> 1

else s ′

> 2

; ˆ s), such that (ˆ s, ˆP ) = merge (e, P 1, P 2), ∆s, H s ⊢ (( T, P, ⊥), s 1) −→ f ield (( T1, P 1, p 1), s ′

> 1

), ∆s, H s ⊢

(( T1, P, ⊥), s 2) −→ f ield (( T2, P 2, p 2), s ′

> 2

), p 1 = p2 = p = ⊥. Here we have P ′ = ˆP , p ′ = ˆ p.By induction, we know that Dom (P1) = Dom (P2) = Dom (P ) = Dom (Hs). In this case, from the rule we directly have that p′ = ⊥. Then, we want to show that Dom (P ′) = Dom ( ˆP ) = Dom (Hs). Here, we know that ˆP is the result of 

merge (e, P 1, P 2). But, from the definition of the merge function, the function either re-turns the same mapping if P1 = P2, and it is clear then that Dom ( ˆP ) = Dom (P ), or it create a new temporary variable t such that ˆP (r, f ) = t, but we know that 

r, f must have existed in the domain of either P1 or P2. Then, we can conclude that 

Dom ( ˆP ) = Dom (P1) = Dom (P2) = Dom (P ) = Dom (Hs). □

Lemma 13.4 establishes the soundness of the field transformation. It states that ex-ecuting the original statement s on a concrete state (∆ , H ) and executing the rewritten statement s′ on an enriched state (∆ ′, H ′) that is P-Heap consistent with H produce 53 the same observable behavior, i.e., the same control state η, return value μ, and path condition ν. Furthermore, the local variable mapping is preserved in the sense that ∆′′ ⊆ ∆′′′ , and the P-Heap consistency relation is maintained after execution, ensuring that the rewritten heap H′′′ remains consistent with the updated field map P ′.

Lemma 13.3. [The judgment →f ield maintains the SSA property] If 

∆s, H s ⊢ (( T, P, p ), s ) −→ f ield (( T ′, P ′, p ′), s ′)

isSSA (s)

then 

isSSA (s′)

Proof Rules field-no-op and field-assign hold trivially since, from the hypothesis, we have 

isSSA (s), and s does not change with the rewriting. Now, consider the rules field-comp, field-if-2 . For these rules, we can use straight-forward induction over the premises of the rules to conclude that the rewritten statement maintains the SSA property. Next, consider the field-put rule, here s = put f ield (r, f, e ), and s′ = t := e. But note here that the newly added variable t, is already fresh (it cannot clash with existing variables), thus it cannot clash with any of the defined/assigned variables. Next, consider the field-get and field-invoke , in both of these rule no new assignments are introduced, thus, the SSA property is maintained there too. Now, consider the more interesting case of field-if-1 . In this case, new statements are added in ˆ s in the form of a sequential composition of assignment statements of the form 

t := γ(e, t 1, t 2). According to the definition of the merge, t must be fresh, i.e., cannot clash with any of the existing variables. Finally, consider the field-if-3 and field-if-4 . In both of these rules, the if-statement is rewritten, where one of its sides is sequentially composed with a sequence of get-field 

statements; i.e., ( s′

> 2

; addP uts (P ′′ )), and ( s′

> 1

> 2

and s1

are both in SSA form by using induction. Moreover, the put-field statements that results from addP uts function, do not define/introduce new assignment statements. Thus, in both cases the rewritten statement also maintains the SSA property. □

P ′, is the same as the domain of P , and we have from the hypothesis that ( P, H ′, ∆′) ⋊ H.Moreover it must be that an existing reference-field pair in P ′ was updated rather than a new one was added. Thus, we can observe here that ∆ ′′′ (t) = v1 = v2 = H′′ (r, f ). Thus, we can also conclude that ( P ′, H ′′′ , ∆′′′ ) ⋊ H′′ .

field-invoke, field-put-var, field-get-var We will show the proof for field-invoke, but all three rules field-invoke, field-put-var , and field-get-var are follow the same proof structure. In general, the proof of this rule follows from the observation that the field-puts statement, generated by addPuts(P) , copies the contents of P into the heap without altering any other 

55 environment variable. By the induction hypothesis, we have ( P, H ′, ∆′) ⋊ H, which implies that if P is non-empty, then for every reference-field pair, the corresponding temporary variable name holds a concrete value equal to that of the reference-field pair in the heap. Consequently, writing back the values of P into H reproduces H itself. Therefore, the invoke 

statement is executed in exactly the same environment as the original statement, without introducing any changes. As a result, the final state remains identical, and thus all conclusions of the lemma continue to hold.

We now lift the soundness result from the recursive field judgment to the top-level wrapper. Theorem 13.5 establishes that executing the original statement s and its field-transformed counterpart s′′ from the same concrete state produce the same observable behavior: the same control state η, path condition ν, and return value 

μ. Furthermore, the local variable mapping is extended consistently, i.e., ∆ ′ ⊆ ∆′′ ,and the heap is preserved, i.e., H′ = H′′ , reflecting that all heap accesses have been internalized as variable assignments by the transformation. 

Theorem 13.5 (Field Transformation is Sound) .

(∆ , H ) |=H (∆ s, H s, π )∆s, H s ⊢ (( I, O, T ), s ) −→ f ield −wrap (( I, O, T ′), s ′′ )Θ ⊢ (( η, ∆, H, ν, μ ), s ) ⇒c (η′, ∆′, H ′, ν ′, μ ′)Θ ⊢ (( η, ∆, H, ν, μ ), s ′′ ) ⇒c (η′′ , ∆′′ , H ′′ , ν ′′ , μ ′′ )

then 

η′ = η′′ ∧ ν′ = ν′′ ∧ μ′ = μ′′ (1) ∆′ ⊆ ∆′′ (2) 

H′ = H′′ (3) 

Proof First, we have by applying the field-wrap that p = ⊥, T ′ = T ∪ Range (P ) such that 

Hs ⊢ (( T ′, P, ⊥), s ) −→ f ield (( T ′′ , P ′, p ), s 2). Next, observe that the obtained s′′ can be understood in three parts, i.e., s′′ = s1; s2; s3,such that s1 = addGets (P ); skip ;, and s3 = skip ; addP uts (P ′). This break down is unique since addGets and addP uts cannot contain a skip -statement.
