# 14 T R 8: Constant Reference Propagation 

The constant reference propagation transformation aims to resolve reference vari-ables by propagating concrete references to subsequent variables in the IR. Fig. 15 shows the rules for the transformation. The rules define two judgments. The judgment 

T ⊢ (∆ r, s ) −→ const ∆′ 

> r

traverses an IR statement s, accumulating mappings from variables to their resolved concrete references in ∆ r , where T is the set of tempo-rary variables whose assignments to concrete references are eligible for collection. The wrapper judgment ∆ s, T ⊢ (( μs, π r), s ) −→ const -wrap (( π′

> r

, μ ′

> s

), s ′) is the entry point of the transformation, triggering the const judgment on s to collect ∆ r, then applying 60 substitution via sub − wrap to replace all occurrences of the collected reference vari-ables throughout s, πr , and μs, producing the rewritten statement s′ and updated π′

> r

and μ′

> s

.resolved concrete references. Rule const-assign-change handles the core case: if a variable x ∈ T is assigned a concrete reference r ∈ R, then ∆ r is updated to record this mapping. Rule const-assign-no-change handles the remaining assignment cases, where either x / ∈ T or the right-hand side is not a concrete reference, leaving ∆ r un-changed. Rules const-if and const-comp propagate the transformation structurally, threading ∆ r through sequential composition while processing both branches of a con-ditional independently. All other statements are handled by const-no-op , which leaves ∆r unchanged. Rule const-wrapper ties the transformation together. It first applies the constant propagation judgment to collect all concrete reference assignments into ∆ r, effectively replacing those assignments with skip while recording their mappings. Once ∆ r is fully populated, the wrapper immediately applies sub-wrap to substitute all occur-rences of the collected reference variables throughout the IR with their corresponding concrete references in ∆ r. This two-phase design ensures that all concrete references are first identified and collected in a single pass, and then propagated uniformly to all their use sites via substitution, resolving previously unresolved reference variables for subsequent transformations such as field elimination and method inlining. Next, rule const-assign-change handles the core case: if a variable x ∈ T is assigned a concrete reference r ∈ R, then ∆ r is updated to record this mapping. Rule const-assign-no-change handles the remaining assignment cases, where either 

x /∈ T or the right-hand side is not a concrete reference, leaving ∆ r unchanged. Rules const-if and const-comp propagate the transformation structurally, threading ∆r through sequential composition while processing both branches of a conditional independently. All other statements are handled by const-no-op , which leaves ∆ r

unchanged. The resulting map ∆ r is then used by the substitution transformation to replace unresolved reference variables with their concrete counterparts in subsequent transformations. Here the noOp const (s) is defined as 

noOp const (return e ) = true noOp const (skip ) = true noOp const (new (z, c )) = true noOp const (put f ield (z, f, e )) = true noOp const (get f ield (x, z, f )) = true noOp const (invoke (x, z, g, −→y )) = true noOp const (s) = f alse otherwise 

noOp const (e1 ⋄ e2) = true noOp const ( e) = true noOp const (γ(e1, e 2, e 3)) = true noOp const (e) = f alse otherwise 61 14.1 Constant Reference Propagation is Sound 

We now establish a key invariant of the constant propagation judgment: the refer-ence map ∆ r remains a subset of the concrete local variable mapping ∆ throughout the transformation. Intuitively, this ensures that every mapping collected in ∆ r corre-sponds to a valid concrete reference assignment witnessed during execution, and that ∆r never introduces mappings that conflict with the symbolic state ∆ s. Lemma 14.1 formalizes this: given that ∆ r ⊆ ∆ holds before rewriting s, it is preserved after rewriting, i.e., ∆ ′ 

> r

⊆ ∆′, and ∆ r remains disjoint from ∆ s.

Proof For assignment statements, two rewriting cases are distinguished: const-assign-change and const-assign-no-change. The former applies when the assigned expression evaluates to a concrete reference value and the target variable is a temporary variable; the latter applies otherwise. Let us consider the first case, i.e., const-assign-change , in this case the rewrite writes to T ⊢ (∆ r , x := r) −→ const ∆′ 

> r

> r

> r

> r

⊆ ∆′

Next consider const-no-change rule. In this case, no change occurs on ∆ r ; i.e., ∆ ′

Thus, we will have (( η, ∆, H, ν, μ ), s 1) ⇒c (η1, ∆1, H 1, ν 1, μ 1), here we have that ∆ ′ = ∆ 1. Recall from Lemma 4.2, it must be that ∆ ⊆ ∆1. We will define the loop invariant to be a property of the state of the rewriting ( ωi,

si), that says, that if we are in the middle of the writing, such that ( ωi, si) is the current rewriting state, then that rewriting is preserving the semantics of the original code, i.e., concretely executing the currently rewritten statement is sound with respect to executing the original statement concretely. We use the superscript i notation to suggest that the loop invariant will hold after i iterations of the fix point loop, in a way that we will define later. More formally, we define the loop invariant be a predicate on the state of the rewriting ( ωi, si), parameterized by the initial concrete state δ, initial symbolic state 

ω and the original statement s.We further define: 

• we write the components of the original concrete state δ as ( η, ∆, H, ν, μ ). 

• state after evaluating the original statement: δ⊥ = ( η⊥, ∆⊥, H ⊥, ν ⊥, μ ⊥), defined by Θ ⊢ (δ, s ) ⇒c δ⊥.

• state after evaluating the rewritten statement after executing the fix-loop i times: 

δi = ( ηi, ∆i, H i, ν i, μ i), defined by Θ ⊢ (δ, s i) ⇒c δi.

• initial state before any rewriting ω = (Θ t, η s, ∆s, H s, μ s, (π, π r, π s), (I, O, T ), u ). 

• state of the rewriting statement after executing the fix-loop ith times: ωi =(Θ it, η is, ∆is, H is, μ is, (πi, π ir, π is), (Ii, O i, T i), u i). The loop invariant is the conjunction of the following properties: 

ηi = η⊥ ∧ Hi = H⊥ ∧ νi = ν⊥ (invariant 1) 

∀o ∈ Oi.∆i(o) = ∆ ⊥(o) (invariant 2) 

μi = ⊥ (invariant 3) 

μ⊥ = evalReturn (∆ i, π ir, μ is) (invariant 4) ∆ |= (∆ is, π i) (invariant 5) (∆ , H ) |=H (∆ is, H is, π i) (invariant 6) 

ηis ⊆ ηi (invariant 7) We prove that the invariant holds at the entry of the fixpoint loop, i.e., before the first iteration. We use superscript 0 to denote the state upon first entering the loop, and superscript i to denote the state after i iterations. The invariant is first established at line 13, which marks the beginning of the loop body, and we show that it holds at this point before any iteration has been executed, i.e., at superscript 0.

## 15.3 Conclusion 

In this paper, we presented the first formal treatment of path-merging symbolic ex-ecution, targeting Java Ranger as a representative tool. We formalized each of its code transformations and proved their soundness with respect to a simplified version of the Java concrete semantics, establishing that the path-merging process preserves program semantics. We hope this work lays the groundwork for future efforts toward formally verifying the correctness of symbolic execution tools more broadly, enforcing rigorous soundness guarantees for tools that are increasingly trusted in safety-critical verification contexts. 

# Appendix A Properties 

Here, we list properties that are shared among semantics. 

Property A.1. For a statement s to be processed by Java Ranger, we assume that the statement obeys the Single Static Assignment property (SSA) for all local variable mappings; i.e., SSA (s), such that the SSA predicate is recursively defined as 

SSA (s1; s2) = ASSN (s1) ∩ ASSN (s2) = ∅ ∧ SSA (s1) ∧ SSA (s2)

SSA (if e then s 1 else s 2; ϕ1(x1, e 11, e 21); · · · ; ϕ(xn, e 1

> n

; e2

> n

)) = (ASSN (s1) ∪ ASSN (s2)) ∩ { x1, · · · , x 2} = ∅ ∧ SSA (s1) ∧ SSA (s2)

SSA (s) = ⊤ otherwise where the ASSN function returns the set of all assigned to variables. Observe that this extends to implicit assignments from the invoke , getfield and new statements that collects the returned value. 

ASSN (x := e) = {x}

ASSN (s1; s2) = ASSN (s1) ∪ ASSN (s2)

ASSN (skip ) = ∅

ASSN (if e then s 1 else s 2; ϕ1(x1, e 11, e 21); · · · ; ϕ(xn, e 1

> n

; e2

> n

)) = 

ASSN (s1) ∪ ASSN (s2) ∪ { x1, · · · , x n}

ASSN (invoke (x, z, g, −→e ))) = {x}

ASSN (return e ) = ∅

74 ASSN (put f ield (z, f, e )) = ∅

ASSN (new (z, c )) = {z}

ASSN (get f ield (x, z, f )) = {x}

Property A.2 (Statement s always has at most a single return within an if-statement) . , i.e., atMostSingleIfReturn (s). Let us define the function 

atM ostSingleIf Return such that atMostSingleIfReturn (s) = ¬hasReturn (s) ∨ singleIfReturn (s)

where singleIfReturn and hasReturn are defined to capture the case where the state-ment s has at most a single return or none at all (see technical report for complete detail [33]) singleIfReturn (s1; s2) = ¬hasReturn (s1) ∧ singleIfReturn (s2)

singleIfReturn (if e 1 then return e 2 else skip ) = true 

singleIfReturn (s) = f alse otherwise hasReturn (return e ) = true 

hasReturn (if e then s 1 else s 2) = hasReturn (s1) ∨ hasReturn (s2)

hasReturn (s1; s2) = hasReturn (s1) ∨ hasReturn (s2)

hasReturn (s) = f alse otherwise 

Property A.3 (Methods in Θ must have a return statement) . All invoked statements in Θ must contain a return statement. More formally, hasReturns (s) must hold. 

Property A.4 (Class object references) . For each class c ∈ C there exists a meta-class cc ∈ C and a class object reference rc ∈ R such that η(rc) = cc and ∆( zc) = rc.We write the class name directly to refer to its class object reference (e.g., Integer denotes zInteger ). Finally, superscripts on environment variables denote successive versions, i.e., ∆1, ∆2, ∆3 abbreviate ∆′, ∆′′ , ∆′′′ rather than exponentiation. 

Property A.5 (Formal argument usage) . All formal arguments of every method body in Θ are used in its statement: 

∀(−→y .s ) ∈ range (Θ) . ∀y ∈ −→y . isUse (y, s )

Property A.6 (No initial concrete return value) . The concrete state cannot be in the middle of returning from a method. More formally, it must be that 

μ = ⊥

75 Property A.7 (ηs = η). The reference typing mapping is carried over unchanged from the concrete semantics, and is never symbolic. 

Property A.8. No symbolic references: all references r ∈ R in ∆s and Hs are concrete. That is, for all (Id, n ) ∈ dom (∆ s), if ∆s(Id, n ) ∈ R then ∆s(Id, n ) is a concrete reference, and similarly for Hs(r, f ) ∈ R.

Property A.9 (Consistent Local Variable Mapping with Symbolic State) . Concrete local variable mapping ∆ is consistent with the symbolic local variable mapping ∆s

with respect to the path condition π.More formally, we have that 

∆ |= (∆ s, π ) ≜ (∆ , π ) ⇝c true ∧ ∀ x ∈ Dom(∆ s).(∆ , ∆s(x)) ⇝c v ∧ v = ∆( x)

Property A.10 (Concrete Heap Consistent with Symbolic State) . Concrete heap H

is consistent with the symbolic local variable mapping ∆s, and symbolic heap Hs with respect to the path condition π.More formally, we have that 

(∆ , H ) |=H (Hs, π ) ≜ ∀(r, f ) ∈ Dom( H).(∆ , H s(r, f )) ⇝c v′ ∧ v′ = H(r, f )

Property A.11 (Symbolic local variable mapping is consistent with respect to the input I). Input variables are not used in any of the mapped expressions in initial ∆s.More formally, if I is defined as I = getInput (s), for some statement s that is going to be summarized, we have that 

∀i ∈ getInputs (s).∀x ∈ Dom (∆ s).¬isU se (i, ∆s(x)) 

Property A.12 (No ϕ statements appear in s).

atMostSingleIfReturn (s) = ¬hasReturn (s) ∨ singleIfReturn (s)where singleIfReturn and hasReturn are defined to capture the case where the statement s has at most a single return or none at all.

singleIfReturn (if e 1 then return e 2 else skip ) = true 

singleIfReturn (s) = f alse otherwise

Property A.14 (If statement s has a return statement then s must have a specific form) . For some statement s′′ , we have that hasReturn (s) = ⇒ (s = s′′ ; if π r then return e else skip ) ∧ πr̸ = f alse 

Property A.15 (πr is true on all return paths) . More formally, If

then

Property A.16 ( statement s′ has no return statements) . More formally, 

¬hasReturn (s′) is valid. 

Property A.17 (Fresh variables have u + 1 suffices) . More formally, (x, ⊥) is in s,then all its occurrences with s′ must have been renamed to (x, u + 1) .

Property A.18 (Old input not in s′). More formally, Old input does not appear, i.e., is not used in s′. More formally if (x, j ) ∈ I, then ¬isU se (( x, j ), s ′)

Property A.19 (Variables in original statement are not indexed) . Given a statement 

s that has not yet been proceed by JR, i.e., JR’s algorithm has not yet run yet, it must be that all variables in s has not been renamed before. More formally for all 

x ∈ isU se (s), then x must be of the form (a, ⊥), where a is the name of the variable and ⊥ location indicates that the variable has not been renamed before. 

Property A.20 (Invoked statements have been prepared and stored in Θ t). This transformation assume that all invoked methods have been transformed and stored in 

Θt, where for each c ∈ C, g ∈ G such that −→y .s = Θ( c, g ), we have a transformed definition of −→y .s of the form (μs, π r ), −→y .s ′ = transf orm (s)(refer to section B for more detail). 

Property A.21 (Reference variables always refer to concrete references) . ∀z.r =∆s(z).r ∈ R, then it must be that r is concrete.
